Until `secrets.enc` exists, `/setup` (`Core\Http\Controller\SetupController`) is gated behind `token.php`, which `bootstrap.php` writes to the document root only after its acceptance gate fully passes. `SetupController` never generates a token itself: if `token.php` is missing, it refuses and displays the exact file content to create over FTP; if present, it compares a submitted value against the file's own content via `hash_equals()`, with session-based progressive lockout (there is no database yet at this point, so `Core\Security\LoginThrottler`'s DB-backed pattern doesn't apply) escalating to a hard stop after ~10 attempts. On successful completion of first-time setup, `token.php` is deleted; a persistent journal entry (and a flash message) warns the operator if deletion fails, since leaving it in place is an unnecessary — though not by itself exploitable — risk.

Collects DB credentials, unit settings (including short name ≤5 chars), email config, initial admin email. Once `secrets.enc` exists, `/setup` reverts to being a normal `superadmin` Configuration page under `RbacGuard`, unchanged — the token gate never applies again.

## 10. Database schema management

No incremental migration files. `schema/core.sql` + each module's `schema.sql` = source of truth, and they are migrated **as one set**, in one pass: `Core\Database\SchemaFiles::all()` returns core first (every module foreign key points into it) then every `modules/*/schema.sql` in a stable order, and every caller that migrates — `public/index.php`, `Task\InstallUpdateHandler`, `Task\RestoreBackupHandler`, `Http\Controller\SetupController`, the end-to-end harness — passes that whole list. Modules used to be migrated separately and lazily by `Module\ModuleManager`, the first time a request noticed a module's manifest `version` was newer than the registry's; a `schema.sql` edit with no accompanying version bump therefore did nothing at all, silently, until a query failed against a column that was never added — and the DDL, when it did run, ran on a visitor's request rather than at deploy time. Every module's schema is in the set whether or not the module is enabled: a disabled module's tables are empty tables, and the alternative is DDL running at the instant somebody enables a module. The ordering among modules is alphabetical, which is only sound while no module's table references another module's — asserted by `Tests\Architecture\ModuleSchemaBoundariesTest` rather than assumed, since such a foreign key would work or fail depending on how two module names happen to sort. `MigrationRunner` compares and generates DDL — this diff never drops a column/table it finds in the database but is no longer declared (data-loss safety net), it only warns. The one narrow exception: a sibling `drops.sql` next to a schema file (e.g. `schema/drops.sql`) can declare reviewed `ALTER TABLE <table> DROP COLUMN <column>;` statements — `MigrationRunner::applyExplicitDrops()` runs each only while the column still exists, so it's idempotent and safe on every request. Still not incremental: once applied everywhere, delete the line from `drops.sql`.

**`migrate()` does not back the database up.** It used to, as its own first step, on every call — a full `ifsnop/mysqldump-php` dump of the entire database, taken before even knowing whether there was any DDL to apply, and once per schema file set (so a release touching six modules produced seven sequential dumps, six of which the next one deleted). It was the single largest cost of a migration, and it duplicated a backup taken moments earlier: the only paths by which a schema file changes on disk are `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` and `Task\RestoreBackupHandler`, which each take their own safety backup first and are the ones that actually roll back, and `bootstrap.php` on a fresh install, where there is nothing to save. What the migration does owe is a record of what it destroyed: `applyExplicitDrops()` writes a `schema_drop_executed` journal entry at `security` level naming the table and the column/constraint, only when a drop genuinely removed something (a line whose column is already gone is skipped before that point, so an installed site does not re-log the whole `drops.sql` on every migration). `MigrationRunner`'s `JournalService` is nullable for the one case where none can exist: a fresh install, whose `event_log` table is created by the very migration being run — and, for now, for the callers that do not yet pass one. `public/index.php` (the live path: the pending-migration check and the migration-step endpoint) is wired; `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` and `Task\RestoreBackupHandler` are not yet, and get theirs in the iteration that makes the update itself run the migration, which rewrites those exact call sites anyway.

**Introspection is read in bulk, once, and memoized on the runner.** The per-table `SchemaIntrospector` methods cost 3 `INFORMATION_SCHEMA` round trips each, and the diff loop called them once per declared table — around 500 round trips on the reference installation (167 tables: 44 core + 123 module). `SchemaIntrospector::getTableDefinitions()` reads `COLUMNS`, `STATISTICS` and `KEY_COLUMN_USAGE` (joined to `REFERENTIAL_CONSTRAINTS`) once each, filtered to the tables asked for, and groups the rows in PHP. Measured on MariaDB 10.11 against the full core schema: **132 queries and 0.187 s become 3 queries and 0.014 s** for the same 44 tables. `CARDINALITY` is deliberately absent from the `STATISTICS` select — reading it makes the storage engine collect index statistics, which is the exact cost being avoided. `MigrationRunner` keeps the result on the instance **beyond a single `migrate()` call**, because `ModuleManager` shares one runner across the core schema and every module's; it is invalidated the moment any DDL executes, since the schema it describes has just changed. The per-table methods stay: `SchemaComparator`, `BackupService` and the tests use them.

**The diff reaches a true "nothing to do" steady state, on both engines.** It did not: a database matching the declared schema exactly still produced **534 `ALTER TABLE ... MODIFY COLUMN`** statements on a full re-diff (139 for `core.sql` alone), regenerated on every migration pass for ever. Nothing failed — the statements succeeded, the hash cache stopped them recurring within a run — which is exactly why it lasted: a permanent cost on every schema change with no error anywhere to point at it, and an `ALTER TABLE` on a populated table is not free. Four causes, all about how a server reports what it stores rather than any real difference, and only one of them was the one previously recorded here. **472**: since MariaDB 10.2 `COLUMN_DEFAULT` holds a SQL *expression*, so a nullable column with no default reports the bare text `NULL`, which read literally means a default of the four-character string "NULL". **60**: MariaDB reports string defaults quoted and escaped (`'public'`, `'it''s'`) where a schema file declares `DEFAULT 'public'` and the parser hands back `public`. **9**: MariaDB has no JSON storage type — it is an alias for `LONGTEXT` plus a CHECK — so a `JSON` column introspects as `longtext`. **2**: `decimal(12, 2)` declared against `decimal(12,2)` reported, one space. The first two are normalised in `SchemaIntrospector::decodeDefault()` rather than in the comparator, and the distinction is the point: `current_timestamp()` versus `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` are two spellings of one *real* default, reconciled only where things are compared, while these two are the introspector reporting something the column does not have — every reader deserves the truth, not only the one that happens to diff. The last two are in `ColumnDefinition::getNormalizedType()`, beside the display-width normalisation that was already there. **`decodeDefault()` has to know which engine it is talking to, and that is not a preference.** MySQL does not quote string literals, so an unquoted `NULL` there means a genuine default of the string "NULL" — the exact opposite of what it means on MariaDB — while "no default" is a real SQL NULL it has already reported unambiguously. Applying MariaDB's reading to MySQL erases a real default; applying MySQL's to MariaDB invents 472. Both readings are live at once, since CI runs MySQL 8 and the reference installation runs MariaDB 10.11. Measured on the full 165-table schema against both: **534 → 0 on MariaDB 10.11, 0 on MySQL 8.0.46**.

**Indexes are diffed by NAME only.** `SchemaComparator` creates any declared index the database lacks (`ADD INDEX`/`ADD UNIQUE INDEX`), but an index that already exists under the declared name is never compared column-by-column — so editing an existing index's column list in `schema.sql` converges on fresh installs and is silently a no-op on every already-installed site, the worst kind of divergence. Redefining an index therefore means declaring it under a NEW name; the superseded one lingers on installed sites (indexes are outside `drops.sql`'s vocabulary, which handles columns and foreign keys only) — harmless beyond a little write overhead, dropped by hand if it matters. Primary-key changes are skipped entirely (`compareTable()` ignores declared `PRIMARY` indexes on existing tables), and foreign keys follow the same name-only matching. The AGENTS.md schema rules carry the working-rule version of this.

**The database is the checkpoint: no queue of pending statements is persisted.** It used to be, and that was a latent way to strand a site forever — the queue was popped *before* the statement ran, so a process killed between an `ADD COLUMN` and its checkpoint came back, replayed it, collected "Duplicate column name", and since any `PDOException` incremented the failure count and the schema hash was only cached at zero failures, the site stayed on the migration-progress page indefinitely. The fix is not a better queue but no queue: every pass re-diffs the live schema and generates exactly what is still missing, which is affordable precisely because introspection is now three queries for the whole schema. A statement that already ran is simply not generated again. Complementing that, error codes meaning "already there" (1050 table exists, 1060 duplicate column, 1061 duplicate key, 1826 duplicate foreign key) are read as benign no-ops rather than failures — the normal shape of a resumed migration, or of two processes racing. A syntax error or a bad type stays a failure.

**Convergence is bounded (`MigrationRunner::CONVERGENCE_ATTEMPTS`, 3).** A pass whose failures are new keeps the attempt open; three consecutive passes failing with exactly the same set of statements is not bad luck but a statement the database will not accept, and the attempt is abandoned: the hash is cached anyway so the site stops serving the progress page, a `schema_drop_executed`-style `security` journal entry is written, and a persistent setting drives a banner on Configuration > Maintenance naming the failing statements (`Core\Database\AbandonedMigration`). The trade — a schema missing a column rather than a site stuck forever — is only defensible because that outcome is made visible; the banner clears itself the moment a later migration converges. `MigrationResult::$converged` carries the distinction to callers, because `$complete` alone cannot: `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` treats a non-converged migration as a failed update and rolls back to its safety backup, which is right where a backup exists, while a visitor arriving on a site nobody is updating gets the degraded-but-serving outcome.

`migrate()` is chunked and resumable, not one uninterruptible pass — a lesson from a real production incident: the previous all-at-once design (introspect+diff+execute every table in one go) could take longer than the host's `max_execution_time` on a large schema change, which killed the request *before* the schema hash was saved, so the identically expensive pass repeated on every subsequent request forever (the site fell over, effectively DoS-ing itself). `migrate()` now does at most `$timeBudgetSeconds` (constructor param, default 20s) of work per call: it checkpoints after every generated DDL statement, persisting a lean `MigrationProgress` as JSON in the same internal `settings` mechanism the schema hash itself uses. What that row carries is deliberately small — since the re-diff landed it holds no queue at all, only accumulated reporting state and the convergence counter, because the live schema IS the state. **The write is rate-limited to one per `MigrationRunner::CHECKPOINT_MIN_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (0.5s), not one per statement**: checkpointing every statement meant 139 `settings` UPSERTs per pass on the reference installation, to persist a value nothing reads until the pass ends. The one write that is never skipped is the one on the way out — a pass leaving because its budget is spent always persists first, whenever it last wrote — so what a skipped write can lose is at most half a second of executed-statement list and progress-bar denominator, never anything a resumed pass needs, since a resumed pass re-derives its work from the database. Hitting the time budget returns `MigrationResult::$complete = false` *without* saving the hash, so the next `migrate()` call for the same schema files resumes from exactly the persisted checkpoint instead of restarting; a schema file changing again mid-attempt (a new content hash) discards the stale progress rather than trying to resume toward a target that no longer exists. A caller that treats "the operation finished" as final (`Task\InstallUpdateHandler`, `Task\RestoreBackupHandler`, `Module\ModuleManager`) must check `$complete` before doing so — none of them bump their own "done" bookkeeping (`update_history.status`, module registry version, etc.) until it's true, otherwise an incomplete migration would be permanently stranded (nothing would ever call `migrate()` for it again). The two background handlers instead reschedule themselves (`SchedulerService::scheduleAfter(..., 0, ...)`) to resume on the next scheduler tick when incomplete.

**One file is one migration, however it is spelled.** The hash cache and the resumable progress row are keyed on the schema file paths, and the callers did not spell the same file the same way: `public/index.php` passed `<root>/public/../schema/core.sql`, `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` and `Task\RestoreBackupHandler` passed `<root>/schema/core.sql`. Two `schema_hash_` rows and two `schema_migration_progress_` rows for one file — so an update finished migrating, cached its hash, and the very next page load asked the other key, still found the old hash there, and served the migration-in-progress page: re-running through the browser the work the update had just done, and resuming neither side's checkpoint. `MigrationRunner` now canonicalises the paths (`realpath()`, falling back to the raw string for a file that does not exist, which `computeSchemaHash()` already treats as empty content) before deriving either key. The end-to-end harness had independently discovered this and worked around it by spelling the path exactly the way `index.php` did; that workaround is gone.

**Three places drive the migration, and none of them is a visitor's page load.** `public/cron.php` migrates the whole declared schema before it processes anything else, through `Database\DeploymentMigration::run()`, with a 900 s budget — a real crontab runs under the CLI SAPI where `max_execution_time` is 0, so a single pass finishes however large the change, with no checkpoint to resume from and nobody waiting on it. It used to build a `MigrationRunner` and never call it, which after §7.3 stopped even satisfying `ModuleManager`'s constructor, and which nothing caught because **no test and no browser ever executes a cron script**: that is why the pass lives in a class rather than inline in the script. An inline block there is unreachable by any check; `Tests\Core\Database\DeploymentMigrationTest` runs the real thing against the real database, budget included. `Http\Controller\MaintenanceController::updateStatus()` runs one short (5 s) slice per poll while an update sits in `migrating`: the administrator watching that page refetches every three seconds anyway, and those requests used to read a status row and do nothing — the same argument as the migration-in-progress page's own endpoint. Only during `migrating` (DDL on a status poll for a failed update would be gratuitous), never fatal (this endpoint's job is to report a status, and the scheduled resume task owns the failure path including the rollback), and it never advances `update_history` — finishing the update stays `Task\InstallUpdateHandler`'s. None of the three is load-bearing on its own: a unit with no crontab and nobody watching is still served by the deploy path and, in the last resort, the migration-in-progress page. The poll's response carries the progress fraction and the install form renders it next to its spinner (`textContent`, never `innerHTML`), because `migrating` is the step that can run for minutes and a spinner alone makes a migration that is advancing look exactly like an update that is stuck.

**A pending migration finishes itself, with nobody watching.** `public/index.php` short-circuits every request while a migration is pending: anything that is not `POST /api/system/migration-step` gets the progress page and exits, before routing, so a visitor never reaches a half-migrated site. That is right, and it had a consequence nobody had followed through: it also swallows the scheduler. `Scheduler\SchedulerKick::now()` — added by IT-07 precisely so a migration would not wait for a visitor — emits an HTTP hop into this same site, and that hop gets the progress page like every other request. On an installation with no real crontab (the reference host has never run `public/cron.php` once, `cron_last_run` is `0`), the only remaining engine was **a human's browser sitting on the progress page**. Production, 2026-08-30, `dev-3fdd425 → dev-00804d3`: the install task finished in 7.3 s at 18:57:49 having queued the resume task for 18:57:48; that task ran at **19:28:04**, thirty minutes and sixteen seconds late, long after `STALE_AFTER_MINUTES` had marked the update failed — and fifty backlogged tasks drained in that same second. `Database\MigrationChain` closes it with the one mechanism that is reachable during the window: the blocked request that was going to show a progress page to nobody starts a chain of fire-and-forget hops onto the migration-step endpoint, and each slice that leaves work behind emits the next. Bounded by its own ceiling setting (zero turns it off, for a `php -S` server that serves one request at a time), restartable once a chain has gone 120 s without a hop so a killed chain does not freeze the mechanism forever, and degrading to exactly the previous behaviour — the progress page's own polling — with no `base_url` or a refused socket. The wiring lives in `MigrationChain::forPendingMigration()` / `afterProgressPage()` / `afterSlice()` rather than inline, for the reason `DeploymentMigration` records about `public/cron.php`: no test and no browser ever enters that branch — the end-to-end harness provisions an install whose schema is already current — so anything left inline there is code nothing can check. `Tests\Architecture\PendingMigrationSelfDriveTest` asserts the three call sites are still there and still follow the responses they belong to, because the defect this replaces passed every functional test, every browser test and a production deploy without going red anywhere.

**One transport for every self-directed request.** `Http\SelfRequest` holds the loopback-first target list, the TLS `peer_name` handling and, above all, the rule that the destination comes from the `base_url` setting and **never** from `HTTP_HOST` or `SERVER_ADDR` — those are attacker-supplied, and on a proxied host trusting them turns any self-request mechanism into a remotely-triggerable SSRF. `Scheduler\SchedulerContinuation` and `Database\MigrationChain` both go through it; a security property duplicated in two places is one that drifts in one of them.

**Two installs can never overwrite the live tree at once.** Until `Maintenance\InstallLock`, nothing serialised them: the protection was indirect and had two holes that lined up. `GitHubWebhookService` skips a push while `UpdateHistoryRepository::findInProgress()` returns a row, but that query deliberately excludes `pending` — a queued install has not touched the site, so it must not gate visitors — which makes a queued install invisible to the guard. And `SchedulerService::cancelPending()` dedupes only within ONE reference, so a push install and a release install (or a manual "Installer maintenant", which is deliberately allowed to force an attempt) are different references and different rows, and `claimOverdue()` claiming each row atomically does not help when they are not the same row. `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` now takes a named `GET_LOCK` — **timeout 0, like every other exclusion lock here** — before `markOtherInProgressAsFailed()` and holds it through backup/download/`installFiles()`; the loser marks its own row `failed` and stands down rather than queueing behind a file copy. The lock is released when `handle()` returns, deliberately not held across an out-of-budget migration: that resumes in a later invocation, on a connection that no longer exists, through `resumeMigration()`, which replaces no files at all.

**A superseded install closes its history row.** Cancelling the scheduled action was all the two webhook supersede points used to do, which left the `update_history` row at `pending` with nothing able to move it: `findInProgress()` and `markOtherInProgressAsFailed()` both exclude `pending` on purpose, and the 15-minute staleness net only looks at rows already running. Observed on scoutmagic.be with two pushes two minutes apart — the maintenance page then showed two updates "En cours" from the same version, which reads exactly like two migrations running in parallel. `GitHubWebhookService::supersedeQueuedInstall()` now closes the row it supersedes, the same thing `MaintenanceController::reconcilePendingScheduledInstall()` already did on the preferences path and for the same stated reason. `Maintenance\AbandonedInstallSweeper`, run from `public/cron.php`, is the net underneath: it closes a `pending` row that no queued or claimed scheduled action points at. The predicate is that absence, never age — a release install waiting for next Monday at 03:00 is legitimately pending for days, and sweeping on age would silently disable the weekly auto-update; age enters only as a floor, so a cron pass landing between `create()` and `schedule()` cannot sweep the row about to be queued.

**Nothing migrates in the process that replaced the files.** `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` and `Task\RestoreBackupHandler` both overwrite the live file tree while running, and both used to migrate immediately afterwards — in that same process, whose own classes are the ones from a moment ago while anything not yet loaded is autoloaded from the new files. That mixture is the mechanism behind six consecutive rollbacks on scoutmagic.be. Each now sets `update_history.status` to `migrating`, schedules the resume task that both already had for the out-of-budget case, and returns. The separation is guaranteed rather than hoped for: `Scheduler\SchedulerRunner::processOverdue()` claims its task list once, at the start of a pass, so a task created during a pass is never run by that pass (`Tests\Core\Scheduler\SchedulerContinuationTest`). `Scheduler\SchedulerKick::now()` then starts that pass immediately, over the same fire-and-forget self-request the scheduler uses to continue a chain (§ scheduler) — without it the migration would wait for the next cron tick or the next visitor, which is the "migrate on somebody's page load" this removes. The kick is opportunistic exactly like a hop: no `base_url`, no secret, no loopback, a refused socket all leave the queue to drain as before. By the time it runs the operation has already succeeded and the migration is queued, so a failed kick is a slower migration, never a failed update. **This is what makes the compatibility shims below removable**: once no installation predates this change, no update can ever again run one version's `MigrationRunner` against another version's `MigrationResult`/`MigrationProgress`.

**`MigrationResult` and `MigrationProgress` carried compatibility shims, and they are gone.** They were the two classes a self-update met in a mixed state: `Task\InstallUpdateHandler` replaced the files and then migrated in the same PHP process, so `MigrationRunner` — already loaded — was the OLD runner, while these two, which nothing constructs on an ordinary request, were autoloaded from the NEW files. Removing a parameter therefore broke the update itself and broke it permanently, since every retry ran the same old code: `MigrationResult::$backupCreated` cost scoutmagic.be six consecutive rollbacks on `Error: Unknown named parameter $backupCreated`, and `MigrationProgress`'s six queue fields would have cost six more on `array_push(): Argument #1 must be of type array, null given`. Both sets were reinstated as inert shims and both have since been removed, which is safe **only because the migration no longer runs in the process that replaces the files** (see above). That is the condition, not the calendar, and `Tests\Architecture\SelfUpdateMigrationBoundaryTest` is what keeps it true: it asserts that the method calling `installFiles()`/`restoreFiles()` never calls `migrate()`, and that it still hands the work to `resumeMigration()`. Re-adding an inline `migrate()` call to either handler compiles, passes every functional test, works perfectly on an installation whose schema is already current — and takes production down permanently the first time a release changes one of those two classes. Until that boundary stops holding, a new field goes at the END of either constructor with a default, never inserted among the existing ones.

Concurrent callers are serialized with a MySQL/MariaDB advisory lock (`GET_LOCK('scoutmagic_schema_migration', 0)`, non-blocking — a caller that doesn't get it immediately just reports "not done yet" rather than doing redundant/racing work); it degrades to no mutual exclusion when `GET_LOCK()` isn't available (this class's local orchestration tests run against SQLite), which is safe there since those tests never call `migrate()` concurrently.

`migrate()` still runs against the whole live schema (no separate deploy step triggers it) so re-introspecting it just to conclude "nothing to do" would be wasted work on every page load between real schema changes — it short-circuits entirely when a SHA-256 hash of the schema file(s) + sibling `drops.sql` matches a hash cached from the last fully-completed, warning-free run (`MigrationRunner::isPending()` exposes just this cheap check, no lock, no work, for a caller that only needs to know whether a migration is outstanding at all). The cache is only written once `$complete` is true and zero statements failed, so a crash or partial failure never gets masked — the next request just resumes (or re-checks in full, if nothing had been checkpointed yet).

**Migration-in-progress page** (`public/index.php` bootstrap, right where `migrate()` used to run inline on every request): when `isPending()` is true, no page is routed normally and no migration work happens inline. A request to `POST /api/system/migration-step` runs exactly one short (5s) `migrate()` chunk and returns `{complete, progress}` as JSON; any other request instead gets a small, self-contained HTML page (inline CSS/JS, no external asset dependency, so it never depends on the rest of the app being routable) showing a progress bar (`MigrationResult::$progressFraction`, a rough two-phase — execute/drops — weighted estimate) whose script calls that endpoint in a fast loop until `complete: true`, then reloads. Closing the tab is safe: the next visitor (or the same one returning later) resumes from whatever `MigrationProgress` was last checkpointed, thanks to the mechanism above — this page is purely a foreground driver for the same resumable `migrate()`, not a separate migration path.

First-time setup also exposes migration directly: the setup wizard's database step runs the real migration itself (`SetupController::installDatabase()`, `POST /setup/install-database`) rather than deferring it to the final form submission, so a slow first schema creation (35+ statements from scratch) gets its own visible step instead of being bundled invisibly into "Installer" — `handleFirstTimeSetup()` still calls `migrate()` itself too, as a defensive no-op safety net, made cheap by the same cache. A fresh install's first schema creation is comfortably inside the default time budget in practice (a single `CREATE TABLE` per declared table, no per-table introspection needed since nothing exists yet), so `SetupController`'s call sites don't need to handle `$complete === false` specially.

## 11. Responsive interface (mobile-first)

Bootstrap 5 compiled files. Mobile-first CSS. Hamburger left, unit name right. Offcanvas from left on mobile; on desktop a horizontal bar whose menus open a **mega-menu panel** — absolutely positioned under the bar (`.desktop-megamenu`, `z-index: 1030`: above page content, below the offcanvas backdrop and the toasts), a CSS grid of titled columns built from `MenuBuilder::build()`'s `groups` (§7.1). One column per declared group, at most four, each a list of text rows carrying the entry's `menu_icon`; per-member entries keep their avatar and subtitle. `public/assets/js/nav.js` opens it on click only — never hover, which a touch laptop cannot express and a keyboard user cannot reach — and closes it on a second click, on `Escape` (focus returns to the trigger), or on a `mousedown` outside `#desktopNav`; `aria-expanded`/`aria-controls` carry the state, and no focus trap is needed since the panel follows its triggers in DOM order. The tab's `active` class is a separate thing from `aria-expanded`: it marks the menu the current page belongs to and is never touched by the script. This replaced a permanent second row of pills that wrapped to three lines at Configuration's nineteen entries and changed height with the active menu. Custom CSS is warranted here — Bootstrap 5 has no multi-column mega-menu component, so nothing is being duplicated. The breadcrumb bar (§8.28) is consequently visible at every width: it is now the only thing on a desktop screen naming the current page's ancestry. Hybrid "Espace membres" menu (dynamic member entries, sorted ahead of core and module pages regardless of order — see §7.1). 44px touch targets. HTML5 input types.

## 13. Code conventions

- Namespace PSR-4: `Core\` → `core/`, `Modules\<ModuleName>\` → `modules/<module_name>/src/`.
- **All code, comments, variable names, table names, column names, commits, PRs: English.**
- **All UI text (labels, messages, Twig content): French.**
- Controller: reads request → calls Service → picks view. No SQL, no business logic.
- Service: never accesses `$_SESSION`/`$_POST` directly.
- Repository: only layer that touches PDO. Prepared statements only.
- View: never calls Repository or Service.
- Compiled Twig templates are cached per installed version (`storage/temp/twig_cache/{version}`, `Core\View\TwigFactory::cacheDirectory()`). Outside debug mode `auto_reload` is off and Twig keys its cache by template *name*, not contents, so a flat directory would keep serving a pre-deployment compiled class forever — the version namespace is what makes a deployment pick up changed templates on its first request, with no cache-clearing step. The one case it doesn't cover, by design: hand-editing a `.twig` file in place without changing `VERSION` (use debug mode for that).

## 14. What Devin must never do

- Call `RbacGuard` from a controller instead of letting the Router do it.
- Write a route in a `module.json` without `role_min`.
- Put business logic or SQL in a Controller or View.
- Duplicate core functionality in a module (auth, session, encryption, journal, mail, scheduler, cookie consent).
- Create an incremental migration file — update the module's `schema.sql`.
- Modify `schema/core.sql` for a module-specific need.
- Write custom CSS that duplicates a Bootstrap component.
- Introduce a frontend build tool (a bundler, a Sass compiler, a transpiler — anything that turns `public/assets/` into a build output). Node-based *test* tooling is not that, and is allowed: see §1 and §15.
- Store personal data in `VARCHAR` — use `BLOB` via `EncryptionService`.
- Write `WHERE` on an encrypted field — use blind index.
- Put personal data in logs, journal, or error messages.
- Link to a file with a direct path — use `file_url(id)`.
- Store uploaded files under `public/`.
- Write a module's own log table — use `JournalService`.
- Use French in code/comments/table names.
- Use English in UI text.
- Set a non-essential cookie without checking `CookieConsentService::isAllowed()`.
- Submit code without corresponding automated tests.

## 15. Tests

Three complementary, independently-runnable automated test stacks:

- **PHPUnit** (`tests/`, mirroring the structure of `core/` and `modules/`) — the server-side test suite: Services, Repositories (against a test database), Controllers/routes (including the RBAC boundary at every `role_min`), and the rest of the PHP application. Five suites, all of them run by a bare `vendor/bin/phpunit`: `Core`, `Modules`, `Bootstrap`, `Security` (`tests/Security/` — the source-level audits AGENTS.md § Security checklist leans on: SQL injection, encryption of personal data, file access, upload MIME trust, response headers, POST size limits) and `Integration` (`tests/Integration/` — scenarios spanning several services at once). **A directory absent from `phpunit.xml` is a suite that does not run**: `Security` and `Integration` existed for a long time without being listed, so eight files silently stopped being executed and drifted; anything added under `tests/` must be reachable from a `<testsuite>` or it is decoration.
- **Vitest + jsdom** (`tests/js/`) — isolated unit tests for first-party browser JavaScript (`public/assets/js/`), run under Node with a simulated DOM (jsdom): no PHP server, no MySQL, no real network. `fetch`, the Service Worker, WebAuthn, etc. are mocked where a script under test touches them. These are development/test tooling only (§1) — they exercise the real production `.js` files directly, never a reimplementation of their logic, and exist to catch regressions in deterministic, DOM-adjacent frontend logic (form validation, client-side computed state) fast and without a browser.
- **Playwright + headless Chromium** (`tests/e2e/`) — end-to-end tests against a real, running ScoutMagic. One canonical command, `npm run e2e` (`scripts/e2e.sh`), provisions a throwaway install (its own `storage/`, its own generated secrets, its own empty database migrated from `schema/core.sql` by the real `Core\Database\MigrationRunner`, and **every** module activated through the real `Core\Module\ModuleManager::activate()` — the instance points its statistics destination at itself, so even the receiver-only module is visible and wired), serves it with `php -S` over the **real** `public/index.php`, drives it with a real browser, and tears all of it down — on success, on failure, and on Ctrl-C. It answers the one question the other two stacks structurally cannot: *does the application boot at all?* PHPUnit instantiates controllers directly and never executes the composition root in `public/index.php`, which is exactly how a `TypeError` on every request once reached production (see AGENTS.md § Static analysis). Every module is activated at provisioning time for the same reason: that composition root wires each enabled module before routing anything, so a module left off is a block of it no scenario executes — and the second time that `TypeError` reached production, it was in a module the harness did not enable. `all-modules-enabled.spec.js` asserts the invariant against the real Modules page, so a module added later is covered the day it lands. Deliberately kept to high-value scenarios only — one per surface where the browser decides what the server receives (hand-built request bodies, chunked uploads, polling loops, flows finished inside an email) or where a rule is only observable between two differently-privileged browsers — never a per-page smoke sweep: this is a merge-blocking CI check and a release gate (`scripts/release.sh`), so determinism matters far more than coverage, and every scenario must prove itself deterministic from a clean state before it lands. Assertions are semantic (ARIA roles, accessible names, the document title) wherever a role or a name identifies the element; the few places where none does fall back to the ids and classes the module's own JavaScript binds to — a contract rather than incidental structure — and say so in the spec that does it. Beyond "does it boot", the suite walks the application's riskiest browser↔server seams (the full inventory lives in README.md § Tests de bout en bout); three scenarios illustrate the recurring shapes. `scout-year-transition.spec.js` replays the whole scout-year changeover — preview, Desk import, staff year, public switch — against the workflow described on `/admin/scout-year`, which is its specification and carries a reminder to that effect (as does `ScoutYearController::buildTransitionSteps()`, where the step wording lives). `gallery-download.spec.js` fills an album with two photos a phone gave the same name, turns the task queue through the instance's own `public/cron.php` (`tests/e2e/support/scheduler.js` — the first scenario to need a background task to have run, since a gallery upload has no renditions until `gallery`/`process_photo` does), and then saves each photo and the album's ZIP from a real browser: whether a click SAVES a file and under WHAT NAME is decided between the `download` attribute and `Content-Disposition`, which PHPUnit and Vitest each see only half of — and the two downloads are checked against EACH OTHER (`Modules\Gallery\Service\MediaFileName`), never against a string written in the test. `groups-discussion.spec.js` writes in a discussion group — a message, a message carrying a link, a poll and a vote, a comment, a reaction, and then a second member commenting so the first is told something is new and can report it, a phone-sized viewport with `groups.js` blocked (the only check anywhere that this module's standing "everything still works with no JavaScript" promise holds), and a comment whose whole content is a photo, previewed before it is sent — because the groups composer is the one place where the BROWSER decides what the server receives: `public/assets/js/groups.js` intercepts the form's submit and hand-builds the request body, so neither PHPUnit (a `$_POST` array the test wrote) nor Vitest (a jsdom form with no server behind it) can see whether that body still carries what the form declares. Three defects lived in exactly that gap and were found by adding the scenario. It needs the super-admin to have a member identity and a name on the account, both hard requirements of `Modules\Groups\Service\GroupAccessService`, which `scripts/e2e-support.php` provisions (`e2e_seed_member_for_admin()`) rather than bypasses — plus a second ordinary member and a section both belong to, since a comment is never new to whoever wrote it and reporting is never offered on your own message; because that fixture is bound to the current scout year, the scenario must run before `scout-year-transition.spec.js` moves the public year — Playwright's alphabetical file ordering is what guarantees it. The harness drops `storage/temp/twig_cache` at provisioning, and `tests/bootstrap.php` does the same for PHPUnit: `TwigFactory` keys compiled templates on VERSION alone with `auto_reload` off whenever `debug` is false — right for production, where an install upgrades by version, and wrong for a checkout that edits templates all day, where it makes both suites assert against a template that is no longer on disk (`Tests\Bootstrap\TwigCacheFreshnessTest` is the guard that keeps the drop happening). Optionally, the run also reports PHP line coverage: with `E2E_COVERAGE=1`, pcov collects inside the `php -S` process through an `auto_prepend_file` (`scripts/e2e-coverage-prepend.php`, one fragment per request) and `scripts/e2e-support.php merge-coverage` folds the fragments into `coverage-e2e.xml`, which SonarQube Cloud merges with PHPUnit's report — the only coverage `public/index.php` ever gets. Canonical documentation: README.md § Tests de bout en bout.

A fourth stack sits beside them, answering a question none of the three asks — **does the running application behave safely on the wire?**

- **OWASP ZAP** (`tests/dast/`, `scripts/dast.sh`) — dynamic application security testing. `./scripts/dast.sh --profile=passive` provisions a throwaway install through **the same `scripts/e2e-support.php provision` the browser suite uses** (never a second copy of it), serves it over self-signed HTTPS, replays the Playwright suite through a ZAP proxy, and fails on any finding at Medium or above. The attack surface is deliberately the browser suite rather than ZAP's spider: a spider cannot follow a magic link out of the browser and back through a mailbox, confirm an address, or register a passkey, and the end-to-end suite already drives all three as several signed-in identities. Two pieces of the harness carry their own reasoning and their own files: `scripts/dast-tls-proxy.php` terminates TLS in front of `php -S` (which speaks none), because the `Secure` cookie flag and HSTS are unobservable in cleartext — it sets `X-Forwarded-Proto`, which the instance believes only through `Core\Http\RequestScheme`'s per-deployment opt-in (SECURITY.md § 9), enabled on that instance and nowhere else; and `scripts/dast-support.php` holds everything needing more than a line of shell, including the check that fails the run when ZAP's site map does not contain the authenticated pages the suite visits. That check exists because the failure it catches is otherwise silent: Chromium bypasses a proxy for loopback addresses unless told not to, and without `--proxy-bypass-list=<-loopback>` every scenario passes, ZAP records nothing, and the report is clean because it examined nothing. `npm run e2e` is strictly unaffected — the proxy, the HTTPS scheme and the widened timeouts are all behind environment variables only `scripts/dast.sh` sets, following the `E2E_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` precedent that was already there. Canonical documentation: README.md § Analyse de sécurité dynamique, and SECURITY.md § 15 for what a scanner can and cannot tell you about this codebase.

Frontend unit tests are a complement to, never a replacement for, PHPUnit's integration tests or the manual mobile (~375px) and desktop (~1280px) visual verification every page/component still requires — they mock the server/browser boundary precisely so they can run in isolation, which is also exactly why they can't substitute for either of those two.

A fourth, non-test mechanism runs alongside these three: JavaScript static analysis (`npm run typecheck`, TypeScript's `checkJs` over `public/assets/js/`, no build step — see `AGENTS.md` § Static analysis and README.md § Analyse statique JavaScript) is the JavaScript equivalent of `vendor/bin/phpstan analyse` — it catches unresolved identifiers, wrong argument counts, and statically-detectable invalid property access *before* any test runs, which is a different guarantee than Vitest exercising behavior at runtime.

Automated tests are mandatory for every feature and must be kept up to date as the codebase evolves. The RBAC guard must have explicit test coverage on every role boundary.
